# The Structure of Almost Stationary Measures

**arXiv ID**: 2608.01466v1
**Authors**: Ilya Gekhtman, Simon Machado, Omri Solan, Yuval Yifrach
**Published**: 2026-08-02
**Categories**: math.DS, math.PR
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2608.01466v1

## Abstract

Let $G$ be a higher-rank simple Lie group acting on a space $X$. A   theorem of Nevo and Zimmer asserts that every ergodic stationary   probability measure on $X$ is either $G$-invariant or admits a projective   factor $G/Q$ for a proper parabolic subgroup $Q$.   We develop a quantitative theory of stationary measures and prove an   effective form of this dichotomy. We introduce notions of $\eps$-almost   stationarity, $δ$-almost invariance and $δ$-almost projective   factor, and show that every $\eps$-almost stationary measure is either   $δ$-almost invariant or carries a $δ'$-almost projective factor,   with $δ,δ'$ explicit in $\eps$ and depending only on $G$. No   ergodicity, arithmeticity or Diophantine hypothesis is imposed, and the   bounds are uniform over all $G$-spaces.   The proof introduces several tools: the \emph{entropigeonhole method}, an   entropy-based pigeonhole principle yielding a quantitative Mautner   phenomenon; \emph{factor functions}, quantitative analogues of functions on   homogeneous factor spaces; and a \emph{fast generation} dichotomy in the   spirit of growth in groups. In a companion paper these are used to show,   among other things, that a discrete subgroup of infinite covolume has   injectivity radius at least $c\log^{(4)}r$ somewhere in the ball of radius   $r$, which is an effective form of a theorem of Frączyk and Gelander.

## Full Text

The Structure of Almost Stationary Measures

## The Structure of Almost Stationary MeasuresIlya Gekhtman, Simon Machado, Omri Solan and Yuval Yifrach

## Abstract

LetGGbe a higher-rank simple Lie group acting on a spaceXX. A
theorem of Nevo and Zimmer asserts that every ergodic stationary
probability measure onXXis eitherGG-invariant or admits a projective
factorG/QG/Qfor a proper parabolic subgroupQQ.

We develop a quantitative theory of stationary measures and prove an
effective form of this dichotomy. We introduce notions ofϵ\epsilon-almost
stationarity,δ\delta-almost invariance andδ\delta-almost projective
factor, and show that everyϵ\epsilon-almost stationary measure is eitherδ\delta-almost invariant or carries aδ′\delta^{\prime}-almost projective factor,
withδ,δ′\delta,\delta^{\prime}explicit inϵ\epsilonand depending only onGG. No
ergodicity, arithmeticity or Diophantine hypothesis is imposed, and the
bounds are uniform over allGG-spaces.

The proof introduces several tools: theentropigeonhole method, an
entropy-based pigeonhole principle yielding a quantitative Mautner
phenomenon;factor functions, quantitative analogues of functions on
homogeneous factor spaces; and afast generationdichotomy in the
spirit of growth in groups. In a companion paper these are used to show,
among other things, that a discrete subgroup of infinite covolume has
injectivity radius at leastc​log(4)⁡rc\log^{(4)}rsomewhere in the ball of radiusrr, which is an effective form of a theorem of Frączyk and Gelander.

## 1Introduction

Invariant measures are the basic object of ergodic theory and a remarkable source of structure, but for actions of
non-amenable groups they are hard to construct and need not exist. A natural
substitute is provided bystationary measures, that is, probability
measures invariant under averaging along a random walk. Unlike invariant
measures, stationary measures come for free: ifμ\muis a probability measure
on a locally compact groupGGacting on a compact spaceXX, then every
weak-∗limit of the Cesàro averagesνn=1n​∑i=0n−1μ∗i∗δx\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mu^{*i}*\delta_{x}(1.1)

isμ\mu-stationary.

Stationarity is therefore cheap, while invariance is the source of rigidity.
All the content of the theory has to be recovered afterwards, from a structure
theorem describing how far a stationary measure can be from an invariant one.
For higher-rank semisimple Lie groups such a theorem is available, and it is
remarkably strong.

## Theorem 1.1(Nevo–Zimmer[NZ]).

Let(X,ν)(X,\nu)be a probability measure space equipped with an action ofGG,
a higher-rank semisimple Lie group without rank-one factors. Ifν\nuisμ\mu-stationary and ergodic, then one of the following holds:
- •

(Invariance)the measureν\nuisGG-invariant;
- •

(Projective factor)there is a parabolic subgroupQ≤GQ\leq Gand aGG-equivariant mapπ:X→G/Q\pi:X\rightarrow G/Qpushingν\nuto the standard stationary measure onG/QG/Q.

The theorem makes no assumption whatsoever on the space acted upon, and
identifies a finite list of algebraically prescribed models accounting for every
obstruction to invariance. The list originates in Furstenberg’s identification
of the Poisson boundary ofGGwith a homogeneous spaceG/QG/Q[FurstenbergPoisson,Furstenberg73]; the content of Theorem1.1is that
these boundaries are not merely a source of stationary measures but the only
one.

Theorem1.1, together with the classification of measure-preserving
actions ofGGdue to Stück and Zimmer[Stuck-Zimmer], reaches geometry
through a recurring pattern. One builds a sequence of measures on the Chabauty
space of closed subgroups ofGGout of geometric data, extracts a weak-∗limit, checks that the limit is stationary, and concludes that it must beδ{e}\delta_{\{e\}}. This is how[Samurai]shows that normalised Betti numbers
of lattices inGGdecay as the covolume grows, and how Frączyk and Gelander[Fraczyk-Gelander]show that a discrete subgroup of infinite covolume has
unbounded injectivity radius in its quotient.

Both conclusions invite a rate. How fast do the Betti numbers decay? How large
an injectivity radius is guaranteed within distancerrof a basepoint? The
method above cannot answer either question, and the obstruction is structural
rather than technical: stationarity is a property of the limit alone, so
whatever quantitative information the approximating sequence carried is lost at
precisely the point where the structure theorem becomes available.

Our starting point is that this loss is unnecessary. The construction
(1.1) is quantitative before any limit is taken: the
measuresνn\nu_{n}are not stationary, but they satisfy‖μ∗νn−νn‖T​V≤2n,\left\lVert\mu*\nu_{n}-\nu_{n}\right\rVert_{TV}\leq\frac{2}{n},(1.2)

by a two-line computation requiring no hypothesis onXXor onμ\mu. This
suggests that the right object of study is not the stationary measure but thealmoststationary one: a measure satisfying‖μ∗ν−ν‖T​V≤ϵ\left\lVert\mu*\nu-\nu\right\rVert_{TV}\leq\epsilon, with stationarity recovered as the limiting
caseϵ=0\epsilon=0. On such measures the classical dichotomy has no meaning as
stated, and the question is whether it has a quantitative shadow, with
conclusions degrading in a controlled way asϵ→0\epsilon\to 0.

It does. This paper is the first of a series on thealmost structure of
measures, and develops a quantitative structure theory for almost stationary
measures under actions of higher-rank semisimple Lie groups. We introduce
effective notions of stationarity, invariance and projective factor, and prove
that almost stationary measures obey a quantitative form of the Nevo–Zimmer
dichotomy. The following informal statement captures the main result; the
precise version is Theorem1.10. Throughout we normalise the action so
that orbit mapsg↦g​xg\mapsto gxare11-Lipschitz, which costs nothing beyond
rescaling the metric onXX.

## Theorem 1.2(Quantitative Nevo–Zimmer, informal).

LetGGbe a higher-rank simple Lie group acting on a metric spaceXX, letμ\mube the time-one heat kernel onGG, and letf:X→ℝf:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}be a11-Lipschitz observable whose sup norm is at most11. There are
constantsC,c>0C,c>0depending only onGGwith the following property.

Letν\nube a probability measure onXXthat isϵ\epsilon-almost stationary,
that is‖μ∗ν−ν‖T​V≤ϵ\left\lVert\mu*\nu-\nu\right\rVert_{TV}\leq\epsilon, and letη∈(0,1]\eta\in(0,1]. Then at
least one of:
- •

(Almost invariance)the distribution offfis almost
unmoved by the unit ball ofGG:W1(f∗(g.ν),f∗ν)≤C(|logϵ|−c+η1/4)for everyg∈G1W_{1}\bigl(f_{*}(g.\nu),\,f_{*}\nu\bigr)\leq C\bigl(\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert^{-c}+\eta^{1/4}\bigr)\qquad\text{for every }g\in G_{1}

whereW1W_{1}denotes the11-Wasserstein distance;
- •

(Almost projective factor)there is a setW⊂XW\subset Xwithν​(W)≥η\nu(W)\geq\eta, a scaleR=R​(ϵ)R=R(\epsilon)tending to infinity and an errorδ=δ​(ϵ)\delta=\delta(\epsilon)tending to0asϵ→0\epsilon\to 0, such that for everyx∈Wx\in Wthe stabilizerstabG⁡(x)\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)is contained, up to an errorδ\deltaand out to scaleRR, in a parabolic subgroupQx≤GQ_{x}\leq G.

The parameterη\etagoverns a genuine trade-off rather than a defect of the
proof: makingη\etasmall strengthens the first alternative and weakens the
second, as it must, since a measure may well be a mixture in which only a small
part sits on a projective factor.

The theorem is already sharp enough for geometry. In the companion paper[QFG]we apply it to the Cesàro averages (1.1) onG/ΓG/\Gamma, forΓ≤G\Gamma\leq Gdiscrete of infinite covolume. Since these
measures are2/n2/n-almost stationary for free, the dichotomy applies at every
finite scale, and one obtains an effective form of the Frączyk–Gelander
theorem: the maximal injectivity radius on the ballBrX/Γ​(o)B_{r}^{X/\Gamma}(o)is at
leastc​log(4)⁡rc\log^{(4)}r, withccdepending only onΓ\Gammaandoo. This answers
the question raised in[Fraczyk-Gelander, Remark 1.4(ii)], and it yields a
geometric characterisation of lattices in higher rank: if the injectivity radius
inX/ΓX/\Gammagrows more slowly thanlog(4)\log^{(4)}, thenΓ\Gammais already a
lattice. The same circle of ideas gives a proof of the Stück–Zimmer theorem that takes about a page. We
return to these in Section1.5.

## 1.1Context

No effective form of Theorem1.1appears to have been known. The
quantitative results available for random walks on homogeneous spaces classify
or equidistribute orbits rather than describe almost stationary measures, and
each of them pays for its rate with an arithmetic or Diophantine hypothesis. In
the work of Bourgain, Furman, Lindenstrauss and Mozes on stiffness for toral
automorphisms[BFLM], quantitative equidistribution holds away from a
Diophantine exceptional set; the effective equidistribution theorems for
unipotent flows of Lindenstrauss, Mohammadi and Wang[LMW]and of
Lindenstrauss, Margulis, Mohammadi and Shah[LMMS]rest on arithmeticity
and spectral gap; the recent theorems of Bénard and He[Benard-He]give
effective local limit and equidistribution statements for random walks on
semisimple groups under moment and Diophantine assumptions.

Theorem1.2imposes no condition of this kind, and this is the feature
we would single out:

the bounds depend only onGG, and are uniform over allGG-spaces
and allϵ\epsilon-almost stationary measures on them.

Nothing in the qualitative theory suggests that such uniformity should be
available. A second constraint on the use of Theorem1.1is the
ergodicity and mixing hypotheses it requires, which can be hard to verify: in
their study of lattice actions on manifolds of low dimension, Brown, Rodriguez
Hertz and Wang[Brown-RodriguezHertz-Wang]observe as much, and establish a
dichotomy of Nevo–Zimmer type by different means. Theorem1.2carries
no ergodicity hypothesis; see Remark1.12.

Read from a distance, this paper belongs to a familiar tradition. Approximate
subgroups stand to subgroups as almost stationary measures stand to stationary
ones, and the structure theory of Breuillard, Green and Tao[BGT]and of
Hrushovski[Hrushovski]shows that a purely approximate hypothesis can
still force an algebraic conclusion; the same is true of almost representations,
where Kazhdan’sϵ\epsilon-representations[Kazhdan-eps]and the stability
results of Becker, Lubotzky and Thom[BLT]recover exact algebraic objects
from approximate ones, and of approximate lattices[Machado-approx]. In
each case the finitary notion turns out to have content of its own rather than
merely approximating the classical one, and the tools built to handle it outlive
the first application. We expect the same here, and
Section1.4is written with that expectation in mind.

## Remark 1.3(On the rate).

The rate we obtain is polynomial in|log⁡ϵ|\lvert\log\epsilon\rvertand is certainly not
optimal; a polynomial dependenceδ​(ϵ)=ϵc\delta(\epsilon)=\epsilon^{c}would be the
natural conjectural target, and is excluded by no example we know. Two
points temper this. First, the rate we prove is already sufficient for the
applications in[QFG]. Second, it is not obvious that any effective
rate should hold. The Cesàro construction yields approximate stationarity
with no hypotheses, but the content of Theorem1.2is effectiverigidity, an explicit dichotomy between invariance and a projective
factor, and quantitative results of that shape normally require algebraic,
arithmetic or Diophantine input. Here none is imposed.

## 1.2Almost stationarity, almost invariance, almost factors

Making the Nevo–Zimmer dichotomy effective requires effective substitutes for
its three protagonists. None of the three has a canonical quantitative
counterpart, and the choices made here determine what the resulting theorem is
good for. Two principles guide them: the quantitative notion should beverifiable in practiceon the measures one actually meets, and shoulddegenerate to the classical notionin the limit. The first two notions
are quickly disposed of; the third is the delicate one.

## 1.2.1Setup and conventions

Throughout the paperGGis a connected noncompact simple Lie group with finite
centre and real rank at least two, andK≤GK\leq Gis a maximal compact subgroup.
We fix a left-invariant Riemannian metric onGGand writeGR={g∈G:d​(e,g)≤R}G_{R}=\{g\in G:d(e,g)\leq R\}. We also fix a faithful finite-dimensional
representation ofGGand writeGR∥G_{R}^{\|}for the correspondingnorm
ballof radiusRR; see Section3. The two families are
related byGc​log⁡R⊂GR∥⊂GC​log⁡RG_{c\log R}\subset G^{\|}_{R}\subset G_{C\log R}, and the reader will
lose nothing on a first pass by readingGR∥G_{R}^{\|}asGlog⁡RG_{\log R}. Norm balls
are the natural scale here because the quantities we control degrade
polynomially in the norm and only exponentially in the Riemannian radius.

By aGG-spacewe mean a metric spaceXXwith a continuous
action ofGGby homeomorphisms whose orbit mapsg↦g​xg\mapsto gxare11-Lipschitz. We insist that the spaceXXneed not be compact in the full generatlity of Theorem1.10. We writeℳ1​(X)\mathcal{M}_{1}(X)for the Borel probability measures onXX,g.νg.\nufor the pushforward ofν\nuunderx↦g​xx\mapsto gx, andμ∗ν=∫Gg.ν​d​μ​(g)\mu*\nu=\int_{G}g.\nu\,d\mu(g)forμ∈ℳ1​(G)\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(G).

## 1.2.2Almost stationarity and almost invariance

Two metrics on measures are used, and the distinction matters. The first is the
total variation norm∥⋅∥T​V\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{TV}, which is insensitive to the metric onXX; the second is the bounded transport distanceW1b​(ν1,ν2)=sup{|ν1​(φ)−ν2​(φ)|:φ∈C​(X),‖φ‖∞≤1,Lip​(φ)≤1},W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=\sup\bigl\{\lvert\nu_{1}(\varphi)-\nu_{2}(\varphi)\rvert:\varphi\in C(X),\ \left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1,\ \mathrm{Lip}(\varphi)\leq 1\bigr\},(1.3)

which is insensitive to sets of small measure. Deleting the constraint‖φ‖∞≤1\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1turnsW1bW^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}into the11-Wasserstein distanceW1W_{1}, by Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality; the two agree up to a factormax⁡(1,12​diam⁡X)\max(1,\tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{diam}X)and coincide on spaces of diameter at most22(Section3). We keep the sup-norm constraint only so thatW1b≤2W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}\leq 2on everyGG-space, which makes the defects in
Theorem1.10comparable across spaces of different diameter; the
reader who prefers to think in terms of transport may readW1bW^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}asW1W_{1}throughout. For measurablef:X→ℝf:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}with‖f‖∞≤1\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1we
writeW1f​(ν1,ν2)=W1b​(f∗​ν1,f∗​ν2)W_{1}^{f}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(f_{*}\nu_{1},f_{*}\nu_{2}). Heref​(X)f(X)has diameter at
most22, so this isexactlythe11-Wasserstein distance between the
distributions of the observableffunderν1\nu_{1}andν2\nu_{2}, which is the form
in which the applications use it.

The definition of almost stationarity is dictated by
(1.2): the correct effective form of stationarity
is the one the Cesàro averages already provide, before any limit is taken.

## Definition 1.4(Almost stationarity and almost invariance).

Letϵ>0\epsilon>0, letν∈ℳ1​(X)\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(X)and letf:X→Yf:X\rightarrow Ybe measurable
with‖f‖∞≤1\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1. We say thatν\nuis
- (a)

ϵ\epsilon-almost stationarywith respect toμ∈ℳ1​(G)\mu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(G)if‖μ∗ν−ν‖T​V≤ϵ\left\lVert\mu*\nu-\nu\right\rVert_{TV}\leq\epsilon;
- (b)

(f,ϵ)(f,\epsilon)-almost invariantifW1f(g.ν,ν)≤ϵW_{1}^{f}(g.\nu,\nu)\leq\epsilonfor everyg∈G1g\in G_{1}.

Variants of the second notion, in whichW1fW_{1}^{f}is replaced byW1bW^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}or by∥⋅∥T​V\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{TV}, are recorded in Section3and used in
the proof.

Almost stationarity is stated in total variation, the strongest reasonable
choice, which makes the hypothesis demanding but is precisely the form in which
(1.2) delivers it.

Almost invariance, by contrast, is stated relative to an observable, and we
insist on this relative form for a concrete reason: in the intended applications
one does not care about the measure itself but about the distribution of a
single geometric quantity (an injectivity radius, a systole, a Betti number
density) and the invariance one can hope to establish is exactly the
invariance of that distribution. Takingf=idf=\mathrm{id}recovers the absolute
statement, but the caseY=ℝY=\mathbb{R}is the one we use.

## Remark 1.5(Why two different metrics).

One would like a dichotomy stated entirely in total variation, and this is
impossible. Total variation is blind to the size of a group element: forν=δx\nu=\delta_{x}one has∥g.ν−ν∥T​V=2\left\lVert g.\nu-\nu\right\rVert_{TV}=2wheneverg​x≠xgx\neq x,
however closeggis to the identity, so no quantitative notion of
invariance survives there. Since orbit maps are11-Lipschitz, the couplingx↦(x,g​x)x\mapsto(x,gx)gives insteadW1b(g.ν,ν)≤W1(g.ν,ν)≤d(g,e)W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(g.\nu,\nu)\leq W_{1}(g.\nu,\nu)\leq d(g,e)for everyggand everyν\nu, which is what makes almost invariance
a meaningful hypothesis, and what makes the normalisation on orbit maps the
right one rather than a technical convenience. ThusW1bW^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}is the coarsest
metric for which the conclusion can hold, and total variation the finest one
in which the hypothesis is available for free from
(1.2): the theorem carries the weakest
hypothesis and the strongest conclusion the problem permits, which is what one
wants from a tool.

## 1.2.3Almost projective factors

The remaining notion is the delicate one, because the existence of a projective
factor is an assertion about an equivariant map, and equivariance is not a
quantitative condition. Our way around this is to replace the factor map by its
shadow on stabilisers.

Supposeπ:(X,ν)→(G/Q,mG/Q)\pi:(X,\nu)\rightarrow(G/Q,m_{G/Q})is aGG-map withQ≤GQ\leq Gparabolic. Forν\nu-almost everyxxwe havestabG⁡(x)⊂stabG⁡(π​(x))\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\subset\operatorname{stab}_{G}(\pi(x)), and writingπ​(x)=g​Q\pi(x)=gQthis readsstabG⁡(x)⊂g​Q​g−1.\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\subset gQg^{-1}.(1.4)

Conversely, (1.4) very nearly characterises the situation: for the
purposes at hand, a measurable assignmentx↦g​Q​g−1x\mapsto gQg^{-1}satisfying
(1.4) is the same thing as a projective factor. And
(1.4) is a containment of subsets ofGG, whichdoesadmit
an honest quantitative relaxation: we ask that the stabiliser be uniformly close
to a conjugate ofQQrather than contained in it.

## Definition 1.6(One-sided Hausdorff distance).

For subsetsA,BA,Bof a metric space,dH+​(A,B)=supa∈Ad​(a,B)d_{H}^{+}(A,B)=\sup_{a\in A}d(a,B).

Let𝒬\mathcal{Q}denote the space of maximal parabolic subgroups ofGG, viewed as a
space of closed subsets ofGGwith the conjugation action. SinceGGhas finite
centre,𝒬\mathcal{Q}is a compactGG-space, a finite disjoint union of the projective
varietiesG/QG/Q.

## Definition 1.7(Almost projective factor).

LetXXbe aGG-space,W⊂XW\subset Xmeasurable, andR,δ>0R,\delta>0. We say
thatWWhas an(R,δ)(R,\delta)-projective factorif there is a
measurable mapπ:W→𝒬\pi:W\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}such that for everyx∈Wx\in WdH+​(stabG⁡(x)∩GR∥,π​(x))≤δ.d_{H}^{+}\bigl(\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G^{\|}_{R},\,\pi(x)\bigr)\leq\delta.(1.5)

WhenW={x}W=\{x\}we say that the action has an(R,δ)(R,\delta)-projective factor
atxx.

## Remark 1.8(The role of the scaleRR).

The truncationstabG⁡(x)∩GR∥\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G_{R}^{\|}is not a technicality. Stabilisers
are typically unbounded, and no quantitative statement can control all of an
unbounded set at once;RRrecords how far out along the stabiliser the
approximation is claimed. Consequently (1.5) is
informative only whenRRis largeandδ\deltais small, and it is
vacuous in either degenerate regime: forRRclose to11the left-hand side
is close to0for anyπ\pi, and forδ\deltacomparable to the diameter
ofGR∥G_{R}^{\|}it holds trivially. The content of Theorem1.10is
that both parameters can be driven into the useful range simultaneously, at
an explicit rate inϵ\epsilon. Note also that the distance is one-sided, so
that (1.5) asks the stabiliser to be almost contained inπ​(x)\pi(x)rather than close to it; two-sided closeness would already fail for
the trivial stabiliser. Finally,π\piis not required to be equivariant,
nor to be defined on all ofXX: in the classical statement equivariance is
automatic once (1.4) holds on a conull set, whereas hereπ\piis produced pointwise on a set of measure bounded below.

## 1.2.4The random walk

Almost stationarity is a hypothesis relative to a step measure, which we now
fix.

## Definition 1.9.

Letptp_{t}denote theKK-bi-invariant heat kernel onGG, that is, the lift
toGGof the heat kernel at timetton the symmetric spaceK\GK\backslash G,
normalised to be a probability density with respect to Haar measure. We setμ:=p1\mu:=p_{1}.

The choice appears less canonical than it is. The heat kernel enters only
through the quantitative Furstenberg decomposition of Section4, and
we expect Theorem1.10to hold for any sufficiently smooth admissible
step measure, the general case following from a local limit theorem
approximating high convolution powers by the heat kernel at an appropriate time,
in the style of Koegler[Koegler]. We do not pursued this here: the paper is
long enough, and the applications in[QFG]are insensitive to the choice.

## 1.3The main theorem

## Theorem 1.10(Almost Nevo–Zimmer).

LetGGbe a connected noncompact simple Lie group with finite centre and
real rank at least two, and letμ\mube as in
Definition1.9. There are constantsC,c,c′>0C,c,c^{\prime}>0depending only onGGwith the following property.

LetXXbe aGG-space, letϵ>0\epsilon>0, and letν∈ℳ1​(X)\nu\in\mathcal{M}_{1}(X)beϵ\epsilon-almost stationary with respect toμ\mu. Letf:X→ℝf:X\rightarrow\mathbb{R}be a11-Lipschitz, bounded by11inL∞L^{\infty}. Then for
everyη∈[0,1]\eta\in[0,1]at least one of the following holds:
- (a)

ν\nuis(f,C​(|log⁡ϵ|−c+η1/4))\bigl(f,\;C(\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert^{-c}+\eta^{1/4})\bigr)-almost invariant;
- (b)

for everyζ>|log⁡ϵ|−1/40\zeta>\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert^{-1/40}there is
a measurableW⊂XW\subset Xwithν​(W)≥η\nu(W)\geq\etahaving an(Rζ,δζ)(R_{\zeta},\delta_{\zeta})-projective factor, whereRζ=|log⁡ϵ|ζ40​dimG,δζ=C​|log⁡ϵ|c′​ζ40​dimG​ζc.R_{\zeta}=\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert^{\frac{\zeta}{40\dim G}},\qquad\delta_{\zeta}=C\,\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert^{\frac{c^{\prime}\zeta}{40\dim G}}\,\zeta^{c}.

The statement carries two free parameters. The first,η\eta, arbitrates between
the two alternatives: decreasing it strengthens the invariance in
(1) while shrinking the set produced in (2), and one
tunes it to the application. The second,ζ\zeta, lives inside (2)
and arbitrates between the two features of an almost factor discussed in
Remark1.8: largerζ\zetabuys a larger scaleRζR_{\zeta}at the
cost of a weaker approximationδζ\delta_{\zeta}. Since (2) is
vacuous unlessRζR_{\zeta}is large andδζ\delta_{\zeta}small at once, the following
is the quickest way to see that both can be driven into the useful range.

## Remark 1.11(Orientation, and the qualitative limit).

FixR≥1R\geq 1andη,δ>0\eta,\delta>0. Choosingζ\zetawithRζ≥RR_{\zeta}\geq R,
which is admissible onceϵ\epsilonis small, givesδζ≪R(log⁡|log⁡ϵ|)−c\delta_{\zeta}\ll_{R}(\log\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert)^{-c}. Hence there isϵ0=ϵ0​(G,R,η,δ)\epsilon_{0}=\epsilon_{0}(G,R,\eta,\delta)such that everyϵ\epsilon-almost stationaryν\nuwithϵ<ϵ0\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}is either(f,C​η1/4+δ)(f,C\eta^{1/4}+\delta)-almost
invariant, or carries a setWWof measure at leastη\etawith an(R,δ)(R,\delta)-projective factor. In particular, lettingϵ→0\epsilon\rightarrow 0returns Theorem1.1, up to the ergodicity discussed in
Remark1.12.

The rate is the point of the exercise, so we record it plainly: the defects in
Theorem1.10decay like a power of|log⁡ϵ|\lvert\log\epsilon\rvert. This is enough
for the geometric applications, where the input measures are almost stationary
at scaleϵ=2/n\epsilon=2/nand the phenomena of interest live at scalelog⁡n\log n. The
logarithm comes from the balance, in Section1.4, between an
entropy budget that forces an exponential time and an almost invariance that
survives only for timeO​(ϵ−1)O(\epsilon^{-1}).

## Remark 1.12(Ergodicity, and its absence).

Theorem1.1assumesν\nuergodic; Theorem1.10assumes
nothing of the kind, and cannot, since there is at present no serviceable
notion of ergodicity for almost stationary measures. What survives is the
role ergodic measures play as building blocks: for almost stationary
measures the natural building blocks are the almost invariant measures and
those carried by almost projective factors, and Theorem1.10should be read as the statement that these two families exhaust the
possibilities. That every almost stationary measure is in fact close to a
convex combination of the two (an effective substitute for the ergodic
decomposition) is proved in subsequent work; see
Section1.5.

## Remark 1.13(Free actions).

Theorem1.10says nothing about free actions, where all
stabilisers are trivial and Definition1.7is vacuous.
That case requires a different quantitative anchor for the factor condition
and is treated separately.

## 1.4Methods: replacing limits by rates

Our proof follows the architecture of[NZ]. This is not a coincidence: we
found that the architecture is rigid while every one of its structural inputs is
not. Each of those inputs is an infinitary statement — a convergence theorem,
an almost-everywhere statement, a statement about closed subgroups — and each
fails outright for measures that are onlyϵ\epsilon-almost stationary. Replacing
them is the content of this paper, and we expect the replacements to outlast the
particular application made here.

## 1.4.1A Furstenberg decomposition with a rate

The classical decompositionν=mG/P∗λ\nu=m_{G/P}*\lambda(appearing in[FurstenbergPoisson]) produces an exactlyPP-invariantλ\lambdaby a martingale limit; for an almost stationary measure
there is no boundary to condition on. We construct the decomposition by hand
instead, tilting the heat kernelhth_{t}by the Poisson kernel at a fixed boundary
point. The resulting measureηt\eta_{t}satisfies the exact identityμt=mK∗ηt\mu_{t}=m_{K}*\eta_{t}, and itsPP-invariance is quantitative:supp∈CdTV​((Lp)∗​ηt,ηt)=O​(t−1/2+θ)\sup_{p\in C}d_{\mathrm{TV}}((L_{p})_{*}\eta_{t},\eta_{t})=O(t^{-1/2+\theta})(Theorem4.2). The tilt is anhh-transform, so equivariance
is exact and the entire error sits in an analytic estimate, supplied by the
global heat-kernel bounds of[APZ]. In effect the Poisson boundary is
replaced by the Gaussian fluctuation of the radial part ofμt\mu_{t}at scalet\sqrt{t}about2​t​ρ2t\rho. This is what ties us to the heat kernel.

## 1.4.2The entropigeonhole principle

The Mautner phenomenon is invoked in[NZ]through the pointwise ergodic
theorem, which is unavailable here: for an almost invariant measure there is no
invariantσ\sigma-algebra and no ergodicity to assume. What the ergodic theorem
provides is that averaging forwards and averaging backwards eventually agree,
and this we recover finitarily. By Pinsker’s inequality applied to the
Jensen–Shannon divergence, any discrepancy between the forward and backward
averages at a given scale is paid for by a gain in Shannon entropy; telescoping
dyadically over scales bounds the total by the available entropylog⁡#​τ\log\#\tau,
which does not grow with the number of scales (Lemma6.8).
Discrepancy therefore cannot be spread across all scales at once, and at a
positive proportion of dyadic times the two averages agree to withinδ\delta.
Combined with the exponential contraction ofVθV_{\theta}unders−ts_{-t}, this
yields the quantitative Mautner phenomenon of
Theorem6.4. The entropy budget forces the relevant
timeTTto be exponential in the precision, while almost invariance survives
only for timeO​(ϵ−1)O(\epsilon^{-1}); balancing the two is the source of the logarithmic
dependence onϵ\epsilonin Theorem1.10.

## 1.4.3Factor functions and fast generation

Applying the above to a test function yields a function almost constant alongVθV_{\theta}-orbits. Classically such a function descends toG/VθG/V_{\theta}; here
there is no quotient, so we retain the function together with the domain on
which its invariance holds, the so-calledfactor functions(Definition5.1). Almost
invariance then propagates along a word at a cost linear in its length, which
means we can afford onlyboundedwords: generation is no longer enough,
it must be fast. Accordingly we prove a dichotomy in the spirit of growth in
groups (Lemma5.7): ifS⊂GR∥S\subset G_{R}^{\|}andV,SV,Sfail
to generateGGinnnsteps, thendH+​(S,V′)≤C​Rc′/ncd_{H}^{+}(S,V^{\prime})\leq CR^{c^{\prime}}/n^{c}for some
proper closedV′≥VV^{\prime}\geq V. Both alternatives are quantitative, and in the same
currency:dH+d_{H}^{+}is exactly what measures almost projective factors in
Definition1.7. The proof is a quantitative escape argument
inspired by a result of de Saxcé[deSaxce]and by the quantitative study
of growth in groups[BGT]. The polynomial dependence matters: the
parameters coming out of the previous step are poor, and only a polynomial
interface absorbs them.

## 1.4.4Assembling

Higher rank enters exactly once, and decisively: the groupsU¯θ\overline{U}_{\theta}generateV¯\overline{V}, and⟨P,V¯⟩=G\langle P,\overline{V}\rangle=G. With this, the
three ingredients fit together asalmost stationary⟶almost​P​-invariant⟶factor functions\displaystyle\text{almost stationary}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{almost }P\text{-invariant}\ \longrightarrow\ \text{factor functions}⟶{almost projective factor,additional almost invariance,\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \begin{cases}\text{almost projective factor,}\\[5.95992pt]
\text{additional almost invariance,}\end{cases}

the last alternative holding for every singular directionθ\theta.

## 1.5Applications and outlook

The applications announced in Section1.5are proved in the
companion paper[QFG], where Theorem1.10is applied to the
Cesàro averages (1.1) onG/ΓG/\GammaforΓ≤G\Gamma\leq Gdiscrete of infinite covolume. Three consequences illustrate the range of the
method: the effective Frączyk–Gelander theorem and the resulting geometric
characterisation of lattices; the criterion thatΓ\Gammais a lattice if and only ifG/ΓG/\Gammacarries a singleϵ0\epsilon_{0}-almost invariant probability measure, with no limiting procedure
anywhere; and a proof of the Stück–Zimmer theorem[Stuck-Zimmer]that
takes about a page and replaces the usual route through the Nevo–Zimmer
theorem, the intermediate factor theorem and amenability arguments. That a
quantitative statement should simplify the proof of the qualitative one is, we
think, the clearest evidence that almost stationarity is the right object of
study rather than a technical surrogate for stationarity.

We regard the present paper as the first step of a longer programme, and several
directions are already underway.
- •

Almost ergodic decomposition.Theorem1.10is a
dichotomy for the measure as a whole. In subsequent work we prove that every
almost stationary measure is close to a convex combination of an almost
invariant measure and a measure carried by an almost projective factor: the
correct effective analogue of ergodic decomposition in a setting where
ergodicity has no quantitative meaning (Remark1.12).
- •

Free actions.A dichotomy covering free actions requires a
quantitative anchor other than stabilisers (Remark1.13), and
will be treated separately.
- •

General step measures.Extending the quantitative Furstenberg
decomposition of Section4to all sufficiently smooth admissible
measures, via a local limit theorem in the style of Koegler’s[Koegler], would extend Theorem1.10with it.
- •

Effective convergence of invariants.The Stück–Zimmer
theorem underlies the asymptotics of Betti numbers along sequences of
lattices[Samurai]. In upcoming work we use the tools developed here to
make Benjamini–Schramm convergence, and with it the convergence of
normalised Betti numbers, effective in terms of the covolume; the result is
a single theorem containing the Frączyk–Gelander[Fraczyk-Gelander], Stück–Zimmer[Stuck-Zimmer]and the seven samurai’s theorems[Samurai].

More broadly, the entropigeonhole method, factor functions and fast generation
are not tied to the Nevo–Zimmer setting. They are quantitative substitutes for
the Mautner phenomenon, for factor maps and for the generation of unipotent
directions, and we expect them to be useful wherever rigidity phenomena in
higher rank are currently accessible only in the limit.

## 1.6Organisation of the paper

Section3fixes notation and collects the facts aboutGG, its
parabolic subgroups and the metrics of Section1.2used
throughout, including the variants of almost invariance omitted from
Definition1.4. Section4proves the
quantitative Furstenberg decomposition. Section6develops the
entropigeonhole principle and the effective Mautner phenomenon.
Section5introduces factor functions, and the fast generation dichotomy.
Theorem1.10is assembled in Section8.

## Statement on the use of AI assistants

The authors used AI assistants (large language models), specifically Claude and Chatgpt in the preparation of
this paper, as follows.

Writing and presentation.AI assistants were used to help draft and
rephrase exposition, to suggest reorganizations of the material, and to
proofread. Every sentence was reviewed, and where necessary rewritten, by the
authors.

Discussion.The authors used AI assistants as a soundboard: to test
formulations, to look for alternative routes through arguments, and to
stress-test claims before committing to them.

Mathematical content.With two exceptions, all mathematical ideas,
statements and proofs in this paper are the authors’ own. The first exception occurs
in Section4. The idea of taking the heat kernel as the step
measure is due to the authors; the idea of twisting the heat-kernel density by
the Poisson kernel, and the computations this leads to in the proof of
Theorem4.2, originated in an exchange with an AI
assistant. The resulting argument was checked line by line by the authors.
The second exception is Appendix D, where we extend the inequality of Gelander-Margulis-Levit to measures of exponential moment. In this case, the authors came up with the idea to do this, and asked Chatgpt to write the text, which was then read line by line and corrected by the authors.
The authors have verified all results and take full responsibility for the
correctness and the content of this paper.

## 4Furstenberg decomposition for almost stationary measures

The first step in the proof of[NZ, Theorem 1]is to decompose the
stationary measureν\nuusing the Furstenberg correspondence; see[NevoZimmer1999, Corollary 1.5]and the original boundary
construction in[FurstenbergPoisson].

## Theorem 4.1(Furstenberg decomposition).

Supposeν\nuis aμ\mustationary measure onXX.
Then there exists aPP-invariant probability measureλ\lambdaonXXsuch that:ν=mG/P∗λ.\nu=m_{G/P}*\lambda.(4.1)

wheremG/Pm_{G/P}is the stationary measure on the maximal boundaryG/PG/P.

For the purpose of our proof, as well as for better understanding the notion of almost stationarity (Definition1.4), it is useful to have a version of this theorem for almost stationary measures and more precisely for measures of the form of Equation (1.1).
Our goal in this section is to prove a quantitative version of this theorem.

## 4.1Setting and statement

We retain the setup and notation fixed in
Section3. ThusS<GS<Gis the chosen maximalℝ\mathbb{R}-split torus,Φ+\Phi^{+}is the chosen positive restricted

and𝔰:=Lie⁡(S)⊂𝔭\mathfrak{s}:=\operatorname{Lie}(S)\subset\mathfrak{p}; thenM=ZK​(S)=P∩KM=Z_{K}(S)=P\cap K. The argument below does not use the assumptionrankℝ⁡G≥2\operatorname{rank}_{\mathbb{R}}G\geq 2.
We follow the conventions of[KnappBeyond, Chapter VI, Sections 2–4]and putρ=12​∑α∈Φ+mα​α.\rho=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\in\Phi^{+}}m_{\alpha}\alpha.

Using the Killing formB𝔤B_{\mathfrak{g}}, equip𝔰\mathfrak{s}with

The standard Iwasawa decomposition states that multiplicationK×S×V⟶G,(k,s,v)⟼k​s​v,K\times S\times V\longrightarrow G,\qquad(k,s,v)\longmapsto ksv,

LetmKm_{K}be Haar probability measure onKK. In the notation of

## Theorem 4.2(Polynomial-rate decomposition).

becausem∈Km\in Kandmmfixesb0b_{0}. It follows thatϑZ\vartheta_{Z}is the unique leftMM-invariant probability measure
onKKliftingaZ.mG/Pa_{Z}.m_{G/P}throughπ−\pi_{-}; uniqueness follows

## Lemma 4.7(Angular concentration under the conditioned measure).

The measureϑZ\vartheta_{Z}is a probability measure, and there arec1,C1>0c_{1},C_{1}>0such that∫KdB​(k​b0,b0)​dϑZ​(k)≤C1​e−c1​0​p​t​(Z).\int_{K}d_{B}(kb_{0},b_{0})\,\,\mathrm{d}\vartheta_{Z}(k)\leq C_{1}e^{-c_{1}0pt(Z)}.(4.25)

By the cocycle identity and theKK-invariance ofmG/Pm_{G/P},𝒥​(k​exp⁡(Z),b0)=𝒥​(exp⁡(Z),k−1​b0).\mathcal{J}(k\exp(Z),b_{0})=\mathcal{J}(\exp(Z),k^{-1}b_{0}).

The second equality is precisely the Radon–Nikodym property of𝒥\mathcal{J}. Now apply Lemma4.5.

For the last assertion, the function(p,b)↦𝒥​(p,b)(p,b)\mapsto\mathcal{J}(p,b)is smooth
and positive onG×BG\times B. Thus, for a compactC⊂PC\subset P, the familyb↦𝒥​(p,b)b\mapsto\mathcal{J}(p,b)is uniformly Lipschitz forp∈Cp\in C. Hence|𝒥​(p,k​b0)−𝒥​(p,b0)|≤LC​dB​(k​b0,b0),\lvert\mathcal{J}(p,kb_{0})-\mathcal{J}(p,b_{0})\rvert\leq L_{C}d_{B}(kb_{0},b_{0}),

and (4.26) follows from
(4.25).

For the exact average, the measure∫Kk.ηt​d​mK​(k)\int_{K}k.\eta_{t}\,\,\mathrm{d}m_{K}(k)has densitypt​(g)​∫K𝒥​(k−1​g,b0)​dmK​(k)\displaystyle p_{t}(g)\int_{K}\mathcal{J}(k^{-1}g,b_{0})\,\,\mathrm{d}m_{K}(k)=pt​(g)​∫K𝒥​(g,k​b0)​dmK​(k)\displaystyle=p_{t}(g)\int_{K}\mathcal{J}(g,kb_{0})\,\,\mathrm{d}m_{K}(k)=pt​(g).\displaystyle=p_{t}(g).

## 4.5Proof of the polynomial rate

Fix a compact setC⊂PC\subset Pandγ∈(0,12)\gamma\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}). LetEtE_{t}be the critical region in
(4.15).

Forp∈Pp\in P, the cocycle identity andp​b0=b0pb_{0}=b_{0}give𝒥​(p−1​g,b0)=𝒥​(p−1,b0)​𝒥​(g,b0)=χ​(p)−1​𝒥​(g,b0).\mathcal{J}(p^{-1}g,b_{0})=\mathcal{J}(p^{-1},b_{0})\mathcal{J}(g,b_{0})=\chi(p)^{-1}\mathcal{J}(g,b_{0}).

Thereforep.ηtp.\eta_{t}is absolutely continuous with respect toηt\eta_{t}, with Radon–Nikodym derivativeRt,p(g):=d(p.ηt)d​ηt(g)=χ(p)−1pt​(p−1​g)pt​(g).R_{t,p}(g):=\frac{\,\mathrm{d}(p.\eta_{t})}{\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{t}}(g)=\chi(p)^{-1}\frac{p_{t}(p^{-1}g)}{p_{t}(g)}.(4.29)

Thus2∥p.ηt−ηt∥T​V=∫G|Rt,p​(g)−1|​dηt​(g).2\left\lVert p.\eta_{t}-\eta_{t}\right\rVert_{TV}=\int_{G}\lvert R_{t,p}(g)-1\rvert\,\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{t}(g).(4.30)

BecauseCCis compact andχ\chiis positive and continuous, there ismC>0m_{C}>0such thatχ​(p)≥mC(p∈C).\chi(p)\geq m_{C}\qquad(p\in C).(4.31)

Ifg=k​exp⁡(Z)​k′∈Etg=k\exp(Z)k^{\prime}\in E_{t}, the heat-kernel ratio estimate
(4.17), withD=CD=C, yields|Rt,p​(g)−1|≤mC−1​|𝒥​(p,k​b0)−χ​(p)|+CC​t−1/2+γ.\lvert R_{t,p}(g)-1\rvert\leq m_{C}^{-1}\lvert\mathcal{J}(p,kb_{0})-\chi(p)\rvert+C_{C}t^{-1/2+\gamma}.(4.32)

We integrate the first term. By Cartan disintegration,

## 4.6The factor supported on the minimal parabolic

It remains to prove the final assertion of4.2. The measureηt\eta_{t}is rightKK-invariant: bothptp_{t}andg↦𝒥​(g,b0)g\mapsto\mathcal{J}(g,b_{0})are rightKK-invariant. Hence it is the unique
rightKK-invariant lift of a probabilityη¯t\bar{\eta}_{t}onG/KG/K.

Iwasawa decomposition givesG=P​KG=PKandP∩K=MP\cap K=M, so the natural mapP/M⟶G/K,p​M⟼p​K,P/M\longrightarrow G/K,\qquad pM\longmapsto pK,

is aPP-equivariant diffeomorphism. Regardη¯t\bar{\eta}_{t}as a probability
onP/MP/M, and define its rightMM-invariant liftσt\sigma_{t}toPPby∫Pf​(p)​dσt​(p)=∫P/M∫Mf​(p​m)​dmM​(m)​dη¯t​(p​M)\int_{P}f(p)\,\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{t}(p)=\int_{P/M}\int_{M}f(pm)\,\,\mathrm{d}m_{M}(m)\,\,\mathrm{d}\bar{\eta}_{t}(pM)(4.41)

for bounded Borelff, wheremMm_{M}is normalized Haar measure onMM.
Thenσt\sigma_{t}is a probability supported onPP, andσt∗mK=ηt.\sigma_{t}*m_{K}=\eta_{t}.

Indeed, both sides are the rightKK-invariant lift toGGof the same
measureη¯t\bar{\eta}_{t}onG/KG/K. Substitutingηt=σt∗mK\eta_{t}=\sigma_{t}*m_{K}into
(4.9) givesμt=mK∗σt∗mK.\mu_{t}=m_{K}*\sigma_{t}*m_{K}.

Finally, invariant lifting fromP/MP/MtoPPis isometric in total
variation. More explicitly, ifσ\sigmais a finite signed measure onP/MP/Mandσ~\widetilde{\sigma}is its rightMM-invariant lift, then‖σ~‖TV=‖σ‖TV.\left\lVert\widetilde{\sigma}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}=\left\lVert\sigma\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}.(4.42)

The inequality “≤\leq” follows immediately by testing against bounded
functions onPPand averaging them overMM; the reverse inequality follows
by testing against functions pulled back fromP/MP/M. The same statement
holds for rightKK-invariant lifting fromG/KG/KtoGG. Since all the
quotient and lifting maps arePP-equivariant,∥p.σt−σt∥T​V=∥p.η¯t−η¯t∥T​V=∥p.ηt−ηt∥T​V.\left\lVert p.\sigma_{t}-\sigma_{t}\right\rVert_{TV}=\left\lVert p.\bar{\eta}_{t}-\bar{\eta}_{t}\right\rVert_{TV}=\left\lVert p.\eta_{t}-\eta_{t}\right\rVert_{TV}.

Thus (4.13) follows from
(4.10), completing the proof of4.2.

## 5Factor Functions and the Fast Generation Dichotomy

This section contains, in a sense, the heart of the main argument of the proof of Theorem1.10.
We start by definingfactor functions, which play the role of the quantitative counterparts of functions on factor spaces, which are central to our argument (and to this of[NZ]).

## Definition 5.1.

LetXXbe aGGspace and letR>0R>0.
LetV≤GV\leq Gbe a subgroup.
Givenϵ>0\epsilon>0and subsetsX′⊂XX^{\prime}\subset X,Y⊂X′×X′Y\subset X^{\prime}\times X^{\prime}, we say that a functionf:X′→ℝf:X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}is a(G/V,R,ϵ)(G/V,R,\epsilon)factor function if for everyx∈Xx\in Xand for everyg∈GR||,v∈V1g\in G_{R}^{||},v\in V_{1}such that(g​x,g​v​x)∈Y(gx,gvx)\in Ywe have:|f​(g​v​x)−f​(g​x)|≤ϵ.\lvert f(gvx)-f(gx)\rvert\leq\epsilon.(5.1)

The setYYis referred to as the factor domain of the factor functionffand is denotedY​(f)Y(f).

The following three definitions and one notation give us the terminology required to discuss existence and properties of factor functions.

## Definition 5.2.

In this definition we use the setup of Definition5.1.
Forg​x,g​v​x∈X′gx,gvx\in X^{\prime}, we say that(x,g,v)(x,g,v)is a factor triple if(g​x,g​v​x)∈Y​(f)(gx,gvx)\in Y(f).
In general, giveng¯=(e,g1,…,gn)∈Gn\overline{g}=(e,g_{1},\dots,g_{n})\in G^{n}andv¯=(e,v1,…,vn)∈V1n\overline{v}=(e,v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in V_{1}^{n}, we say that(x,g¯,v¯)(x,\overline{g},\overline{v})is a factor tuple if for every0≤k≤n0\leq k\leq nwe have(v1​g1​⋯​gk​x,v1​g1​⋯​gk​vk+1​x)∈Y(v_{1}g_{1}\cdots g_{k}x,v_{1}g_{1}\cdots g_{k}v_{k+1}x)\in Y.

## Definition 5.3.

LetV≤GV\leq Gbe a closed subgroup, letS⊂GS\subset Gbe a symmetric finite subset containinge∈Ge\in Gand letn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}.
We say thatV,SV,SgenerateGGinnnsteps if(V1||​S)n⊃G1.(V_{1}^{||}S)^{n}\supset G_{1}.(5.2)

## Notation 5.4.

SupposeV,SV,SgenerateGGinnnsteps.
Letg∈G1g\in G_{1}, and writeg=v1​s1​⋯​vn​sng=v_{1}s_{1}\cdots v_{n}s_{n}(which is possible sinceV,SV,SgenerateGGinnnsteps). Denotes¯g=(e,s1,…,sn)∈S×n\overline{s}_{g}=(e,s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\in S^{\times n}andv¯g=(e,v1,…,vn)∈V1×n\overline{v}_{g}=(e,v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\in V_{1}^{\times n}.
Note that we denote the set ofnn-tuples of elements fromV1V_{1}byV1×nV_{1}^{\times n}to distinguish it from the product (in the group) ofV1V_{1}with itselfnntimes.

## Definition 5.5.

SupposeV,SV,SgenerateGGinnnsteps.
GivenY⊂X×XY\subset X\times Xandx∈Xx\in X, we define the subsetG1​(Y,x)G_{1}(Y,x)by:G1​(Y,x)={g:g∈G1​and​(x,s¯g,v¯g)​is a factor tuple for​Y​for some​s¯g,v¯g​as in5.4}.\displaystyle G_{1}(Y,x)=\{g:g\in G_{1}\text{ and }(x,\overline{s}_{g},\overline{v}_{g})\text{ is a factor tuple for }Y\text{ for some }\overline{s}_{g},\overline{v}_{g}\text{ as in \ref{nota: generation}}\}.(5.3)

The following lemma, which proves extra invariance of factor functions, is where factor functions are shown to be a natural counterpart to functions on factor spaces.

## Lemma 5.6.

LetR>0R>0andn∈ℕn\in\mathbb{N}. Supposeffis a(G/V,R2​n,ϵ)(G/V,R^{2n},\epsilon)-factor function on someX′⊂XX^{\prime}\subset Xand suppose that for somex∈X′x\in X^{\prime}, it holds thatV,stabG⁡(x)∩GR||V,\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G_{R}^{||}generateGGinnnsteps and denoteY=Y​(f)Y=Y(f).
Then for everyg∈G1​(Y,x)g\in G_{1}(Y,x)we have:|f​(g​x)−f​(x)|≤n​ϵ.\lvert f(gx)-f(x)\rvert\leq n\epsilon.(5.4)

## Proof.

By the assumption thatV,stabG⁡(x)∩GR||V,\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G_{R}^{||}generateGGinnnsteps, we can writeg=v1​γ1​⋯​vn​γng=v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{n}\gamma_{n}for somevi∈V1v_{i}\in V_{1}andγi∈stabG⁡(x)∩GR||\gamma_{i}\in\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G_{R}^{||}.
Moreover, by the assumption thatg∈G1​(Y,x)g\in G_{1}(Y,x), we may choosev¯=(e,v1,…,vn)\overline{v}=(e,v_{1},\dots,v_{n})andγ¯=(e,γ1,…,γn)\overline{\gamma}=(e,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})such that(x,γ¯,v¯)(x,\overline{\gamma},\overline{v})is a factor tuple (recall Definition5.2)

Since(x,γ¯,v¯)(x,\overline{\gamma},\overline{v})is a factor tuple, it follows that for everyk=0,…,n−1k=0,\dots,n-1we have:(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​x,v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​vk+1​x)∈Y.(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}x,v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}v_{k+1}x)\in Y.(5.5)

Sinceffis a(G/V,R2​n,ϵ)(G/V,R^{2n},\epsilon)-factor function, by Definition5.1forgk=v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk∈GR2​ng_{k}=v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}\in G_{R^{2n}}andv=vk+1v=v_{k+1}(which can be applied by Eq. (5.5)), we have:|f​(gk+1​x)−f​(gk​x)|=|f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​vk+1​x)−f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​x)|≤ϵ.\lvert f(g_{k+1}x)-f(g_{k}x)\rvert=\lvert f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}v_{k+1}x)-f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}x)\rvert\leq\epsilon.(5.6)

Sinceγk+1∈stabG⁡(x)\gamma_{k+1}\in\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x), we can also write:f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​vk+1​x)=f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​vk+1​γk+1​x)f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}v_{k+1}x)=f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}v_{k+1}\gamma_{k+1}x)(5.7)

so that|f​(gk​vk+1​x)−f​(gk​x)|=|f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​vk+1​γk+1​x)−f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vk​γk​x)|≤ϵ.\lvert f(g_{k}v_{k+1}x)-f(g_{k}x)\rvert=\lvert f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}v_{k+1}\gamma_{k+1}x)-f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{k}\gamma_{k}x)\rvert\leq\epsilon.(5.8)

Using the triangle inequality and the above equation fork=1,…,nk=1,\dots,nwe can deduce that|f​(g​x)−f​(x)|=|f​(v1​γ1​⋯​vn​γn​x)−f​(x)|≤n​ϵ\lvert f(gx)-f(x)\rvert=\lvert f(v_{1}\gamma_{1}\cdots v_{n}\gamma_{n}x)-f(x)\rvert\leq n\epsilon(5.9)

as desired.
∎

## 5.1Fast generation

The following geometric lemma concludes this section by giving a restrictive condition to not having fast generation - the main condition used in Lemma5.6to attain extra invariance.
This condition will be the pivot for the dichotomy of Theorem1.10as displayed in its proof in Section8.

a compact set containingee.

## Proposition 5.9(envelope).

There areC1,b1>0C_{1},b_{1}>0, depending only onGGandVV, with the following
property. LetR≥2R\geq 2, let1≤k≤D−11\leq k\leq D-1, lett∈[0,1]t\in[0,1]and letE∈Grk​(𝔤)E\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})satisfymV​(E)≤tm_{V}(E)\leq t. Ifε∗:=C1​R​tb1<1\varepsilon_{*}:=C_{1}Rt^{b_{1}}<1, then there isU∈𝒳kVU\in\mathcal{X}_{k}^{V}withsupg∈𝒜R,t​(E)dalg​(g,HU)≤ε∗.\sup_{g\in\mathcal{A}_{R,t}(E)}d_{\mathrm{alg}}(g,H_{U})\leq\varepsilon_{*}.(5.12)

## Proof.

Letℬk\mathcal{B}_{k}be the set of triples(u,t,ε)∈(0,1/2]×[0,1]×[0,1](u,t,\varepsilon)\in(0,1/2]\times[0,1]\times[0,1]for which there
existsE∈Grk​(𝔤)E\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k}(\mathfrak{g})withmV​(E)≤tm_{V}(E)\leq tand∀U∈𝒳kV∃g∈𝒜u−1−1,t(E):∀h∈G,Ad(h)U=U⟹dalg(g,h)≥ε.\forall U\in\mathcal{X}_{k}^{V}\ \ \exists g\in\mathcal{A}_{u^{-1}-1,\,t}(E):\quad\forall h\in G,\ \ \operatorname{Ad}(h)U=U\Longrightarrow d_{\mathrm{alg}}(g,h)\geq\varepsilon.(5.13)

The membershipg∈𝒜u−1−1,t​(E)g\in\mathcal{A}_{u^{-1}-1,t}(E)is expressed by the
polynomial inequalitiesu​‖Ad⁡g‖≤1−uu\|\operatorname{Ad}g\|\leq 1-u,u​‖Ad⁡g−1‖≤1−uu\|\operatorname{Ad}g^{-1}\|\leq 1-uanddGr​(Ad⁡(g)​E,E)≤td_{\mathrm{Gr}}(\operatorname{Ad}(g)E,E)\leq t; hence (5.13) is a first-order
formula over the reals andℬk\mathcal{B}_{k}is semialgebraic by
Tarski–Seidenberg. LetKk:=ℬk¯K_{k}:=\overline{\mathcal{B}_{k}}, a compact

## 6Quantitative Mautner phenomenon

## 6.1Statement of main result

Our goal in this section is to prove a quantitative version of Mautner phenomenon.
This will be our main tool for constructing factor functions in Section7.

We start by giving a quantitative sense to Mautner phenomenon as follows.

## Definition 6.1(GG-Lipschitz).

SupposeGGacts on a spaceXXandf:X→Yf:X\rightarrow Yis some function to a metric space(Y,dY)(Y,d_{Y}).
GivenL>0L>0, we say thatffisG,LG,L-Lipschitz if for everyx∈Xx\in Xand for everyg∈G1g\in G_{1}we havedY​(f​(g​x),f​(x))≤L​|g|d_{Y}(f(gx),f(x))\leq L|g|.

## Definition 6.2(QM-phenomenon).

Let(st)t≥0⊂S+(s_{t})_{t\geq 0}\subset S_{+}(recall Definition3.2) be some one-parameter subgroup and letVVbe the expanded horosphere forss.

Suppose we have aPP-spaceXXand aP,1P,1-Lipschitzf:X→Yf:X\to Yfor some metric spaceYY.
Suppose also thatλ\lambdais a probability measure onXX.

Letκ,δ>0\kappa,\delta>0be small numbers and letT>0T>0be large. We say thatλ\lambdahas the(κ,δ,T,f)(\kappa,\delta,T,f)-quantitative Mautner phenomenon for(st)t(s_{t})_{t}(shorthanded(κ,δ,T,f)(\kappa,\delta,T,f)-QM for(st)t(s_{t})_{t}) if there exists a subsetX0⊂XX_{0}\subset Xsuch thatλ​(X0)≥1−κ\lambda(X_{0})\geq 1-\kappaand for everyx∈X0x\in X_{0},v∈Vv\in Vsuch thatv​x∈X0vx\in X_{0}:W1f​(⨏0Tδst​x​𝑑t,⨏0Tδst​v​x​𝑑t)≤δ.W_{1}^{f}(\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}x}dt,\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}vx}dt)\leq\delta.(6.1)

## Remark 6.3(The meaning of quantitative Mautner phenomenon).

The above definition, which may seem technical, is meant to directly quantify the standard Mautner phenomenon.
Namely, forPP-invariant measuresλ\lambda, it follows from the pointwise ergodic theorem that forλ\lambdaalmost everyx∈Xx\in Xand for everyv∈V1v\in V_{1}the limiting measureslimT→∞⨏0Tδst​v​x​𝑑t\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}vx}dt(6.2)

are all equal (independent ofvv).
Quantifying this property means the following things:
- (a)

ReplacingPP-invariant measuresλ\lambdabe norm almostPP-invariant measures;
- (b)

Replacing the statement "forλ\lambda-almost everyx∈Xx\in X" by "for highλ\lambda-measure of points inXX. This is measured using the parameterκ\kappa;
- (c)

Replacing the limit averageT→∞T\rightarrow\infty(in the above equation) by a "stopped average", measured using the parameterTT;
- (d)

Replacing the equalitieslimT→∞⨏0Tδst​v​x​𝑑t=limT→∞⨏0Tδst​x​𝑑t\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}vx}dt=\lim_{T\rightarrow\infty}\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}x}dtfor everyv∈V1v\in V_{1}, byffproximity, measured in the inequalityW1f​(⨏0Tδst​x​𝑑t,⨏0Tδst​v​x​𝑑t)≤δW_{1}^{f}(\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}x}dt,\fint_{0}^{T}\delta_{s_{t}vx}dt)\leq\delta.

Now that we have a definition of the quantified property, we can state and prove the following theorem, saying that this property occurs for the right choice of parameters.

## Theorem 6.4(Quantitative Mautner Phenomenon).

Letλ\lambdabe a normϵ\epsilon-PP-invariant probability measure on aPP-spaceXX.
Let(st)t≥0⊂S+(s_{t})_{t\geq 0}\subset S_{+}be some one-parametric subgroup and letVVbe the expanded horosphere forss.
Letf:X→Yf:X\to Ybe aPP-11-Lipschitz function for some metric spaceYY.
Letδ>0\delta>0be a small number.
Letnnbe theδ/10\delta/10-covering number ofYY.
Letm>0m>0be a large natural number such that for everyk≥m/2k\geq m/2κ=\displaystyle\kappa=((3​m2​log⁡n)−1/4+1m1/2​(32​log⁡n)2)1/2+2m​ϵ​is close enough to​0,\displaystyle((\tfrac{3m}{2}\log n)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m^{1/2}}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log n)^{2})^{1/2}+2^{m}\epsilon\text{ is close enough to }0,δ/10>\displaystyle\delta/10>((m​log⁡n)−1/2+e−k/2),\displaystyle((m\log n)^{-1/2}+e^{-k/2}),p=\displaystyle p=1−((3​m2​log⁡n)−1/4+1m1/2​(32​log⁡n)2)1/2​is greater than​1/2.\displaystyle 1-((\tfrac{3m}{2}\log n)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m^{1/2}}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log n)^{2})^{1/2}\text{ is greater than }1/2.

Then there exists a subset𝒜⊂[1,m−1]\mathcal{A}\subset[1,m-1]of proportion at leastppsuch that for everyk∈𝒜∩[m/2,m]k\in\mathcal{A}\cap[m/2,m], the measureλ\lambdais(κ,δ,2k,f)(\kappa,\delta,2^{k},f)-Quantitative Mautner.

## 6.2Proof

Letτ\taube a partition ofYYintonndisjoint measurable sets such that each setA∈τA\in\tauhas diameterdiam​(A)≤δ10Lip​(f){\rm diam}(A)\leq\frac{\delta}{10\ {\rm Lip}(f)}.
The proof is based on an "entropy-pigeon-hole (entropigeonhole) argument".

## Definition 6.5.

Given an operatorTTon a spaceXXand a natural numbernn, we denoteℰTn\mathcal{E}_{T}^{n}to be the measure on⟨T⟩\left<T\right>given by1n​∑i=0n−1δTi\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\delta_{T^{i}}.
Moreover, we denoteℰT∓n=12​n​∑i=1−nn−1δTi\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp n}=\tfrac{1}{2n}\sum_{i=1-n}^{n-1}\delta_{T^{i}}.

The proof has two steps:
- (a)

The first stepis to reverse the direction of the average into the flowsts_{t}fort≥0t\geq 0to the flowsts_{t}fort≤0t\leq 0;
- (b)

The second stepis to prove that when averaging in the reverse direction (namely according to the flowsts_{t}fort≤0t\leq 0), the distributions of the flow originating atxxand atv​xvxare close to each other.

## First step: reversing the direction of average

## Claim 6.6.

For every two probability vectorsP,Q∈ℝkP,Q\in\mathbb{R}^{k}0≤2​(H​(P+Q2)−H​(P)+H​(Q)2)≥sup{|P(A)−Q(A)|:Ais measurable}.0\leq\sqrt{2\left(H\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right)-\frac{H(P)+H(Q)}{2}\right)}\geq\sup\{|P(A)-Q(A)|:A\text{ is measurable}\}.(6.3)

## Proof.

The quantityH​(P+Q2)−H​(P)+H​(Q)2H\left(\frac{P+Q}{2}\right)-\frac{H(P)+H(Q)}{2}in the square root is termed the Jensen–Shannon divergence, and is larger than the minimum of the Kullback–Leibler divergences of the two orders. Now the inequality follows from Pinsker’s Inequality (see[pinskerBook, Chapter 2, Theorem 6].
∎

Recall Definition3.4and Notation3.3.

## Remark 6.7.

The reversing of direction is achieved using the following key inequality, which can be thought of as a pigeon hole argument for the entropy.
Roughly, every time there is a significant difference between the forward average measure and the backward average measure, there is, by Pinkser’s inequality (see Claim6.6), a growth in entropy.
Since the entropy is bounded, the total growth of distances, measured by the left hand side in the key inequality, has to be bounded.

## Lemma 6.8(Entropigeonhole principle).

LetTTan action ofℝ\mathbb{R}onXX. Then for everyx0∈Xx_{0}\in Xandm≥0m\geq 0,12m​∑n=02m−1∑k=0m−1dτ​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0,ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)2≤2​log⁡#​τ\frac{1}{2^{m}}\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}})^{2}\leq 2\log\#\tau

## Proof.

Fixx0∈Xx_{0}\in X.
By Claim6.6,12m\displaystyle\frac{1}{2^{m}}∑n=02m−1∑k=0m−1dτ​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0,ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)2\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}})^{2}≤22m​∑n=02m−1∑k=0m−1(H​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0)−H​(ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)+H​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0)2)\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\left(H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)-\frac{H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)+H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)}{2}\right)≤22m​∑k=0m−1∑n=02m−1(H​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0)−H​(ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)+H​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0)2)\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\left(H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)-\frac{H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)+H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)}{2}\right)

that we will estimate by a telescoping sum argument. Let us start by considering the negative contributions separately.

Fork≥1k\geq 1andn≥0n\geq 0, we have the identitiesH​(ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)=H​(ℰT∓2k−1∗δTn+2k−1​x0)H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)=H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k-1}}*\delta_{T^{n+2^{k-1}}x_{0}}\right)

andH​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0)=H​(ℰT∓2k−1∗δTn−2k−1​x0).H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)=H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k-1}}*\delta_{T^{n-2^{k-1}}x_{0}}\right).

Hence, by a change of indices−∑n=02m−1H​(ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)+H​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0)2≤−∑n=2k−12m−2k−1H​(ℰT∓2k−1∗δTn​x0).-\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\frac{H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)+H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)}{2}\leq-\sum_{n=2^{k-1}}^{2^{m}-2^{k-1}}H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k-1}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right).

This estimate yields22m​∑k=0m−1∑n=02m−1(H​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0)−H​(ℰT−2k∗δTn​x0)+H​(ℰT2k∗δTn​x0)2)\displaystyle\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{n=0}^{2^{m}-1}\left(H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)-\frac{H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)+H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)}{2}\right)≤22m​∑k=0m−1∑n∈[0;2k)∪(2m−2k;2m−1]H​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0).\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{n\in[0;2^{k})\cup(2^{m}-2^{k};2^{m}-1]}H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right).

And sinceH​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0)≤log⁡#​τH\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)\leq\log\#\taufor every choice ofkkandnn,22m​∑k=0m−1∑n∈[0;2k−1)∪(2m−2k−1;2m−1]H​(ℰT∓2k∗δTn​x0)\displaystyle\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\sum_{n\in[0;2^{k-1})\cup(2^{m}-2^{k-1};2^{m}-1]}H\left(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{\mp 2^{k}}*\delta_{T^{n}x_{0}}\right)≤22m​∑k=0m−12k+1​log⁡#​τ\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{2^{m}}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}2^{k+1}\log\#\tau≤2​log⁡#​τ.\displaystyle\leq 2\log\#\tau.

∎

## Second step: proximity of averages in the reverse direction

## Definition 6.9(sθs_{\theta}-averages).

For everyT>0T>0and for everyGG-spaceXX, we define the measureℰsθT\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}onGGbyℰsθT​(ϕ)=1T​∫0Tϕ​(sθt)​𝑑t.\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}(\phi)=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\phi(s_{\theta}^{t})dt.(6.4)

Note that this definition is simply the continuous variable version of Definition6.5.

## Remark 6.10.

From this point forwards, we will use the continuoussθs_{\theta}averages.
The passage from the discrete version to the continuous version is standard, and we will not include it here.

## Claim 6.11.

For every two probability measuresλ1,λ2\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}onXX, we haveW1f​(λ1,λ2)≤2​δ/5+2​dτ​(f∗​λ1,f∗​λ2)W_{1}^{f}(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\leq 2\delta/5+2d_{\tau}(f_{*}\lambda_{1},f_{*}\lambda_{2})

## Proof.

For everyA∈τA\in\tausample a pointpA∈Ap_{A}\in A.
Letφ:Y→[0,1]\varphi:Y\to[0,1]be a11-Lipschitz function such thatW1b​(f∗​λ1,f∗​λ2)=|∫φ​df∗​λ1−∫φ​df∗​λ2|.W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(f_{*}\lambda_{1},f_{*}\lambda_{2})=\left|\int\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{1}-\int\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{2}\right|.

ThenW1b​(f∗​λ1,f∗​λ2)\displaystyle W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(f_{*}\lambda_{1},f_{*}\lambda_{2})=|∫φ​df∗​λ1−∫φ​df∗​λ2|\displaystyle=\left|\int\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{1}-\int\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{2}\right|≤∑A∈τ|∫Aφ​df∗​λ1−∫Aφ​df∗​λ2|\displaystyle\leq\sum_{A\in\tau}\left|\int_{A}\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{1}-\int_{A}\varphi{\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{2}\right|≤∑A∈τ|∫A(φ−φ​(pA))​df∗​λ1|+|∫A(φ−φ​(pA))​df∗​λ2|+|f∗​λ1​(A)−f∗​λ2​(A)|​φ​(pA)\displaystyle\leq\sum_{A\in\tau}\left|\int_{A}(\varphi-\varphi(p_{A})){\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{1}\right|+\left|\int_{A}(\varphi-\varphi(p_{A})){\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{2}\right|+|f_{*}\lambda_{1}(A)-f_{*}\lambda_{2}(A)|\varphi(p_{A})≤∑A∈τ|∫Adiam​(A)​df∗​λ1|+|∫Adiam​(A)​df∗​λ2|+|f∗​λ1​(A)−f∗​λ2​(A)|\displaystyle\leq\sum_{A\in\tau}\left|\int_{A}{\rm diam}(A){\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{1}\right|+\left|\int_{A}{\rm diam}(A){\rm d}f_{*}\lambda_{2}\right|+|f_{*}\lambda_{1}(A)-f_{*}\lambda_{2}(A)|=∑A∈τ2​diam​(A)​(f∗​λ1​(A)+f∗​λ2​(A))+|f∗​λ1​(A)−f∗​λ2​(A)|\displaystyle=\sum_{A\in\tau}2\,{\rm diam}(A)(f_{*}\lambda_{1}(A)+f_{*}\lambda_{2}(A))+|f_{*}\lambda_{1}(A)-f_{*}\lambda_{2}(A)|≤2​δ/5+2​dτ​(f∗​λ1,f∗​λ2)\displaystyle\leq 2\delta/5+2d_{\tau}(f_{*}\lambda_{1},f_{*}\lambda_{2})

where in the last inequality we used Claim3.5for the measuresμi:=∑A∈τf∗​λi​(A)​δA,i=1,2.\mu_{i}:=\sum_{A\in\tau}f_{*}\lambda_{i}(A)\delta_{A},i=1,2.

and therefore we can deduce that:W1b​(ℰsθ−1R∗δx,ℰsθ−1R∗δv​x)≤2​R−1/2+e−R1/2W^{\mathrm{b}}_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}^{-1}}^{R}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}^{-1}}^{R}*\delta_{vx})\leq 2R^{-1/2}+e^{-R^{1/2}}(6.8)

as desired.

Ifλ\lambdais some probability measure onXX, then there exists a numbern=0,.…,2m−1n=0,.\dots,2^{m}-1and a subset𝒜⊂{0,…,m−1}\mathcal{A}\subset\{0,\dots,m-1\}of proportion at least1−((32​log⁡#​τ)−1/4+1m​(32​log⁡#​τ)2)1/21-((\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{2})^{1/2}such that for everyk∈𝒜k\in\mathcal{A}there exists a subsetX0​(k)⊂XX_{0}(k)\subset Xofλ\lambda-measure at least1−((32​log⁡#​τ)−1/4+1m​(32​log⁡#​τ)2)1/21-((\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{2})^{1/2}such that for everyx∈X0​(k)x\in X_{0}(k)we have:dτ​(ℰsθ2k∗δsθn​x,ℰsθ−2k∗δsθn​x)≤(3​m2​log⁡#​τ)−1/2.d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{s_{\theta}^{n}x},\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{s_{\theta}^{n}x})\leq(\tfrac{3m}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/2}.(6.10)

such that for everyx∈X0​(k)x\in X_{0}(k):dτ​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT−2k∗δx)≤(3​m2​log⁡#​τ)−1/2.d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})\leq(\tfrac{3m}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/2}.(6.11)

Now we have everything we need to prove Theorem6.4.

## Proof of Theorem6.4.

By Corollary6.15applied toT=sθT=s_{\theta}, there exists a subset𝒜⊂[m−1]\mathcal{A}\subset[m-1]of measure at least1−((32​log⁡#​τ)−1/4+1m1/2​(32​log⁡#​τ)2)1/21-((\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m^{1/2}}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{2})^{1/2}such that for everyk∈𝒜k\in\mathcal{A}there existsX0​(k)⊂XX_{0}(k)\subset Xofλ\lambda-measure at least1−((32​log⁡#​τ)−1/4+1m1/2​(32​log⁡#​τ)2)1/2−ϵ​2m1-((\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m^{1/2}}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{2})^{1/2}-\epsilon 2^{m}such that for everyx∈X0​(k)x\in X_{0}(k), we have that:dτ​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT−2k∗δx)≤(3​m2​log⁡#​τ)−1/2.d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})\leq(\tfrac{3m}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/2}.(6.12)

To show the quantitative Mautner property as stated in the theorem, suppose thatx∈X0​(k)x\in X_{0}(k)andv∈V1v\in V_{1}are such thatv​x∈X0​(k)vx\in X_{0}(k).
Applying the above equation forxxand forv​xvx(which is therefore possible), we can obtain:W1f​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT2k∗δv​x)≤W1f​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT−2k∗δx)+W1f​(ℰT2k∗δv​x,ℰT−2k∗δv​x)\displaystyle W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{vx})\leq W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})+W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{vx},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{vx})+W1f​(ℰT−2k∗δv​x,ℰT−2k∗δx)\displaystyle+W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{vx},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})≤4​δ/5+dτ​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT−2k∗δx)+dτ​(ℰT2k∗δv​x,ℰT−2k∗δv​x)\displaystyle\leq 4\delta/5+d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})+d_{\tau}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{vx},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{vx})+W1f​(ℰT−2k∗δv​x,ℰT−2k∗δx)\displaystyle+W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{vx},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x})≤4​δ/5+2​(3​m2​log⁡#​τ)−1/2+W1f​(ℰT−2k∗δv​x,ℰT−2k∗δx).\displaystyle\leq 4\delta/5+2(\tfrac{3m}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/2}+W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{vx},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{-2^{k}}*\delta_{x}).

By Claim6.12, we know that the last term in the above inequality is bounded above bye1−k/2+e−2k/2≤3​e−k/2e^{1-k/2}+e^{-2^{k/2}}\leq 3e^{-k/2}and by our assumption onδ\deltawe obtain:W1f​(ℰT2k∗δx,ℰT2k∗δv​x)≤4​δ/5+2​(3​m2​log⁡#​τ)−1/2+3​e−k/2≤δ.W_{1}^{f}(\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{x},\mathcal{E}_{T}^{2^{k}}*\delta_{vx})\leq 4\delta/5+2(\tfrac{3m}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/2}+3e^{-k/2}\leq\delta.(6.13)

Sinceλ​(X0​(k))≥1−((32​log⁡#​τ)−1/4+1m​(32​log⁡#​τ)2)1/2−ϵ​2m\lambda(X_{0}(k))\geq 1-((\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{m}(\tfrac{3}{2}\log\#\tau)^{2})^{1/2}-\epsilon 2^{m}we deduce the desired statement of the theorem.
∎

## 7Factor Functions via the Quantitative Mautner Phenomenon

## 7.1Preparation and notation

## Notation 7.1.

LetGGbe a simple Lie group of rank at least two. Recall thatΔ\Deltadenotes the set of positive roots ofGG. From now on, we fixθ⊂Δ\theta\subset\Deltaas in Definition3.2. Letsθs_{\theta}be a singular direction in the Cartan subgroup associated withΔ\Delta. DenoteG−G^{-}to be the subgroup of elements ofGGnot expanding under conjugation bysθs_{\theta}.
Similarly, we denoteG+=VθG^{+}=V_{\theta}.

DenoteCθ=CG​(sθ).C_{\theta}=C_{G}(s_{\theta}).(7.1)

In this notation, we haveG−=Cθ​V¯θ.\displaystyle G^{-}=C_{\theta}\overline{V}_{\theta}.(7.2)

We will exploit a decomposition of elements ofGGinto a part inG−G^{-}and one inG+G^{+}. WhenG=SLn⁡(ℝ)G=\operatorname{SL}_{n}(\mathbb{R})and the chosen Cartan subgroup is the subgroup of diagonal matrices, this decomposition is related to the well-knownLU\mathrm{LU}decomposition. While not all elements ofGGcan be decomposed as a product of an element ofG−G^{-}and an element ofG+G^{+}, almost all can; see ClaimA.4.

We will use this decomposition where it exists:

## Definition 7.2.

By ClaimA.4, there exists a proper submanifoldℳ⊂G\mathcal{M}\subset Gsuch that every elementg∈G∖ℳg\in G\setminus\mathcal{M}can be written in the formg=g−​g+g=g_{-}g_{+}whereg−∈G−g_{-}\in G^{-}andg+∈G+g_{+}\in G^{+}.
Given a general elementg∈G∖ℳg\in G\setminus\mathcal{M}, we denoteg+,g−g_{+},g_{-}to be these unique elements.

## Notation 7.3.

For this section, we fix aGG-spaceXXwith a normϵ\epsilon-PP-invariant measureλ\lambdaon it.
Also, we fix a Lipschitz functionf:X→I=[−1,1]f:X\rightarrow I=[-1,1], one choice of positive rootθ\theta, and use the notationG+G^{+}to denoteVθV_{\theta}andG−G^{-}to denote the groupCθ​V¯θ=V¯θ​CθC_{\theta}\overline{V}_{\theta}=\overline{V}_{\theta}C_{\theta}.

## 7.2Construction of factor functions and almost invariance

In this section, we will use Theorem6.4to construct and analyze properties of factor functions.
In order to use Theorem6.4we need to recall the following definition.

## Definition 7.4.

Given a Lipschitz functionf:X→If:X\rightarrow IwhereI⊂ℝI\subset\mathbb{R}is a compact interval,R>0R>0and a diagonal elements∈Ss\in Swe define a functionf^R:X→Lip⁡((Cθ)R||→I)\hat{f}_{R}:X\rightarrow\operatorname{Lip}((C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}\rightarrow I)by[f^R​(x)]​(cθ)=f​(cθ​x)​for everycθ∈(Cθ)R||\displaystyle[\hat{f}_{R}(x)](c_{\theta})=f(c_{\theta}x)\text{ for every $c_{\theta}\in(C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}$ }(7.3)

and denoteYR:=Lip⁡((Cθ)R||→I)\displaystyle Y_{R}:=\operatorname{Lip}((C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}\rightarrow I)(7.4)

to be the space of Lipschitz functions, endowed with the metric of theL∞L^{\infty}-distance.
Forϕ:I→ℝ\phi:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}andf:X→If:X\rightarrow Iwe denoteϕf=ϕ∘f\phi_{f}=\phi\circ f.

The next lemma explains how to the effective Mautner phenomenon from Theorem6.4to construct factor functions.

## Lemma 7.5(Factor functions from quantitative Mautner).

LetR>1R>1, letκ,δ,T>0\kappa,\delta,T>0and suppose thatλ\lambdais a(κ,δ,T,f^R)(\kappa,\delta,T,\hat{f}_{R})-QM probability measure onXX.
Letϕ:I→ℝ\phi:I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}be a11-Lipschitz function such that‖ϕ‖∞≤1\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1.
Then the functionℰT​(ϕf)​(x):=ℰsθT∗δx​(ϕf)\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{T}(\phi_{f})(x):=\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}*\delta_{x}(\phi_{f})

is a(G/Vθ,R,O​(R2​e−T1/2+4​T−1/2+δ))(G/V_{\theta},R,O(R^{2}e^{-T^{1/2}}+4T^{-1/2}+\delta))-factor function.
Moreover, we can chooseX′′⊂XX^{\prime\prime}\subset Xsuch thatλ​(X′′)≥1−κ\lambda(X^{\prime\prime})\geq 1-\kappaand the factor domain ofℰT​(ϕf)\mathcal{E}^{T}(\phi_{f})satisfiesY​(ℰT​(ϕf))={(g−​x′′,g−​v​x′′):x′′,v​x′′∈X′′​and​g−∈(Cθ)R||​(V¯θ)R||,v∈V1||}.Y(\mathcal{E}^{T}(\phi_{f}))=\{(g_{-}x^{\prime\prime},g_{-}vx^{\prime\prime}):x^{\prime\prime},vx^{\prime\prime}\in X^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }g_{-}\in(C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}(\overline{V}_{\theta})_{R}^{||},v\in V_{1}^{||}\}.(7.5)

## Proof.

Assume thatλ\lambdais(κ,δ,T,f^R)(\kappa,\delta,T,\hat{f}_{R})-QM.
Suppose that(g​x,g​v​x)∈{(g−​x′′,g−​v​x′′):x′′,v​x′′∈X′′​and​g−∈(Cθ)R||​(V¯θ)R||,v∈V1||}.\displaystyle(gx,gvx)\in\{(g_{-}x^{\prime\prime},g_{-}vx^{\prime\prime}):x^{\prime\prime},vx^{\prime\prime}\in X^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }g_{-}\in(C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}(\overline{V}_{\theta})^{||}_{R},v\in V_{1}^{||}\}.

Then there existx′′,v​x′′∈X′′​and​g−∈(V¯θ)R||​(Cθ)R||,v∈V1||x^{\prime\prime},vx^{\prime\prime}\in X^{\prime\prime}\text{ and }g_{-}\in(\overline{V}_{\theta})_{R}^{||}(C_{\theta})_{R}^{||},v\in V_{1}^{||}such that|ℰT​ϕf​(g​v​x)−ℰT​ϕf​(g​x)|\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(gvx)-\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(gx)|=|ℰT​ϕf​(g−​v​x′′)−ℰT​ϕf​(g−​x′′)|\displaystyle=|\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(g_{-}vx^{\prime\prime})-\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(g_{-}x^{\prime\prime})|=|ℰsθT∗δg−​v​x′′​(ϕf)−ℰsθT∗δg−​x′′​(ϕf)|\displaystyle=|\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}*\delta_{g_{-}vx^{\prime\prime}}(\phi_{f})-\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}*\delta_{g_{-}x^{\prime\prime}}(\phi_{f})|

where the last equality follows by the definition ofℰT\mathcal{E}^{T}.
Writeg−=cθ​v¯θg_{-}=c_{\theta}\overline{v}_{\theta}wherev¯θ∈(V¯θ)R||\overline{v}_{\theta}\in(\overline{V}_{\theta})_{R}^{||}andcθ∈(Cθ)R||c_{\theta}\in(C_{\theta})_{R}^{||}.

In the equality(∗)(*)below we invoke Definition6.2.
At first sight, there is a mismatch, since the quantitative Mautner property is assumed for the mapf^R:X→YR,\hat{f}_{R}:X\to Y_{R},

whereas the expression appearing here involves the observablecθ−1​ϕf=ϕf∘cθ−1.c_{\theta}^{-1}\phi_{f}=\phi_{f}\circ c_{\theta}^{-1}.

We now explain why the definition nevertheless applies.

Define a functionξθ:YR→ℝ\xi_{\theta}:Y_{R}\to\mathbb{R}

byξθ​(h):=ϕ​(h​(cθ−1)),h∈YR.\xi_{\theta}(h):=\phi\bigl(h(c_{\theta}^{-1})\bigr),\qquad h\in Y_{R}.

Sinceϕ\phiis11-Lipschitz and evaluation at a point is11-Lipschitz onYR=Lip⁡((Cθ)R∥→I)Y_{R}=\operatorname{Lip}((C_{\theta})^{\|}_{R}\to I)equipped with the supremum metric, the mapξθ\xi_{\theta}is also11-Lipschitz.

Moreover, for everyx∈Xx\in X,ξθ​(f^R​(x))=ϕ​(f^R​(x)​(cθ−1))=ϕ​(f​(cθ−1​x))=(cθ−1​ϕf)​(x).\xi_{\theta}(\hat{f}_{R}(x))=\phi\!\left(\hat{f}_{R}(x)(c_{\theta}^{-1})\right)=\phi\!\left(f(c_{\theta}^{-1}x)\right)=(c_{\theta}^{-1}\phi_{f})(x).

Thus the observablecθ−1​ϕfc_{\theta}^{-1}\phi_{f}is obtained fromf^R\hat{f}_{R}by
post-composition with the11-Lipschitz functionξθ\xi_{\theta}.

Since Definition6.2allows post-composition off^R\hat{f}_{R}with arbitrary11-Lipschitz test functions, we may apply it toξθ\xi_{\theta}. It therefore follows thatℰsθT∗δv​x​(cθ−1​ϕf)andℰsθT∗δx​(cθ−1​ϕf)\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}*\delta_{vx}(c_{\theta}^{-1}\phi_{f})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{E}_{s_{\theta}}^{T}*\delta_{x}(c_{\theta}^{-1}\phi_{f})

areδ\delta-close, which is precisely the estimate used in the tenth equality.

Note that sincev¯θ∈(V¯θ)R||\overline{v}_{\theta}\in(\overline{V}_{\theta})_{R}^{||}and sincesθ−1s_{\theta}^{-1}contractsV¯θ\overline{V}_{\theta}we have|sθT1/2​v¯θ​sθ−T1/2|≤R​e−T1/2|s_{\theta}^{T^{1/2}}\overline{v}_{\theta}s_{\theta}^{-T^{1/2}}|\leq Re^{-T^{1/2}}and so the term insideO​(⋅)O(\cdot)can be replaced byδ+4​‖ϕf‖∞​T−1/2+R2​e−T1/2\delta+4\left\lVert\phi_{f}\right\rVert_{\infty}T^{-1/2}+R^{2}e^{-T^{1/2}}.

Subtracting the right hand side from the left in the above equation and recalling the definition of factor functions5.1proves the statement of the claim.

∎

## Notation 7.6.

In the notation of the Lemma7.5, forg=g−​g+∈G1∖ℳg=g_{-}g_{+}\in G_{1}\setminus\mathcal{M}, ifx∈X′′x\in X^{\prime\prime}andg+​x∈X′′g_{+}x\in X^{\prime\prime}, then|ℰT​ϕf​(g​x)−ℰT​ϕf​(g−​x)|≤R2​e−T1/2+4​T−1/2+δ.\lvert\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(gx)-\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(g_{-}x)\rvert\leq R^{2}e^{-T^{1/2}}+4T^{-1/2}+\delta.(7.6)

## Proof.

Sincex,g+​x∈X′′x,g_{+}x\in X^{\prime\prime}andg−∈G−g_{-}\in G^{-}, we have by the last part of Lemma7.5,(g−​g+​x,g−​x)∈Y​(ℰT​ϕf)(g_{-}g_{+}x,g_{-}x)\in Y(\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}).
Now we can apply the above lemma to deduce thatℰT​ϕf\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}is a factor function forY​(ℰT​ϕf)Y(\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}), and by Definition5.1forg=g−,x=x,v=g+g=g_{-},x=x,v=g_{+}we are allowed to deduce that|ℰT​ϕf​(g−​g+​x)−ℰT​ϕf​(g−​x)|≤R2​e−T1/2+4​T−1/2+δ\lvert\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(g^{-}g^{+}x)-\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(g^{-}x)\rvert\leq R^{2}e^{-T^{1/2}}+4T^{-1/2}+\delta(7.7)

as desired.
∎

Suppose that for someT>0T>0the measureλ\lambdais(κ,δ,T,f^R2​n)(\kappa,\delta,T,\hat{f}_{R^{2n}})-QM wheref,f^R2​nf,\hat{f}_{R^{2n}}is as in Lemma7.5.

Thus, and sinceG+,stabG⁡(x)∩GR||G_{+},\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G_{R}^{||}generateGGinnnsteps, by Lemma5.6and sinceR′=R2​nR^{\prime}=R^{2n}we know that for everyg∈G1​(YR​(f),x)g\in G_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x)we have|ℰT​ϕf​(g​x)−ℰT​ϕf​(x)|≤n​(R′⁣2​e−T1/2+4​T−1/2+δ)\lvert\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(gx)-\mathcal{E}^{T}\phi_{f}(x)\rvert\leq n(R^{\prime 2}e^{-T^{1/2}}+4T^{-1/2}+\delta)(7.10)

## 7.3Analysis of the invariance set

## Remark 7.12(Goal and organization of this subsection).

In this section we analyze the setsG^1​(YR​(f),x)\hat{G}_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x), and show that they are rich in the sense of containing fine metric nets insideG1G_{1}.
The proof of Lemma7.13- where we establish this fine metric nets - is highly technical and involved.
To simplify the reading, we have divided its proof into steps, each in a different sub-subsection.
This organization is postponed till after the statement of the lemma.

## Lemma 7.13.

Suppose thatλ\lambdais(κ,δ,T,f^R2​n)(\kappa,\delta,T,\hat{f}_{R^{2n}})-QM.
Assume further that(κ+ϵ)1/2​Rn∈(0,1)(\kappa+\epsilon)^{1/2}R^{n}\in(0,1).
There exists a subsetX′′′⊂X′′X^{\prime\prime\prime}\subset X^{\prime\prime}such thatλ​(X′′′)≥1−R−n/2−5​(κ+ϵ)1/2​Rn\lambda(X^{\prime\prime\prime})\geq 1-R^{-n/2}-5(\kappa+\epsilon)^{1/2}R^{n}and for everyx∈X′′′x\in X^{\prime\prime\prime}such thatG+,stabG⁡(x)∩GR||G_{+},\operatorname{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G^{||}_{R}generateGGinnnsteps, the setG^1​(YR​(f),x)\hat{G}_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x)contains a(2​R−n2​dim⁡(G)+(κ+ϵ)1/2​R4​n)(2R^{-\frac{n}{2}\operatorname{dim}(G)}+(\kappa+\epsilon)^{1/2}R^{4n})-net inG1G_{1}.

## Remark 7.14.

In all the remaining sub-subsections, we assume the conditions of Lemma7.13.

## Remark 7.15(Idea of the proof).

The purpose of Lemma7.13is to show that the setG^1​(YR​(f),x)\widehat{G}_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x)is
large – in the sense of containing a fine net inG1G_{1}– wheneverG+=VθG_{+}=V_{\theta}together withstabG​(x)∩GR∥\mathrm{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G^{\|}_{R}generatesGGinnnsteps.
Combined with Corollary7.11, this allows us to promote the approximate invariance
of the averaged functionℰT​(ϕf)\mathcal{E}^{T}(\phi_{f})fromG^1​(YR​(f),x)\widehat{G}_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x)to a
quantitatively dense subset of the whole unit ballG1G_{1}. We preview the argument
below; its three steps occupy the three sub-subsections that follow.

We first record, in Section7.3.1, an explicit criterion for membership inG^1​(YR​(f),x)\widehat{G}_{1}(Y_{R}(f),x). The generation assumption lets us write eachg∈G1g\in G_{1}as an alternating product of elements ofstabG​(x)∩GR∥\mathrm{stab}_{G}(x)\cap G^{\|}_{R}and ofV=G+V=G_{+}; the intermediate products of such an expression are recorded by the mapsψk,g,x\psi_{k,g,x}andρk,g,x\rho_{k,g,x}of Definition7.16. Definition7.17then
introduces the setsEk,RψE^{\psi}_{k,R},Ek,RρE^{\rho}_{k,R}andEx,Rρ,ψE^{\rho,\psi}_{x,R},
which encode that the plus-parts of these intermediate products carryxxinto the
good setX′′X^{\prime\prime}while their minus-parts remain in the controlled region(Cθ)R∥​(V¯θ)R∥(C_{\theta})^{\|}_{R}(\bar{V}^{\theta})^{\|}_{R}. Claim7.19shows that this suffices:
if(v1′,…,vn′)∈Ex,Rρ,ψ​(g′)(v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{n})\in E^{\rho,\psi}_{x,R}(g^{\prime})theng′∈G^1​(Y,x)g^{\prime}\in\widehat{G}_{1}(Y,x),
which is the implication (7.17).

The measure-theoretic input is supplied in Section7.3.2. Using the largeness ofX′′X^{\prime\prime}and the normϵ\epsilon-PP-almost invariance ofλ\lambda, Claim7.21produces a setX′′′⊂X′′X^{\prime\prime\prime}\subset X^{\prime\prime}of large measure on which most elements ofVRn∥V^{\|}_{R^{n}}returnxxtoX′′X^{\prime\prime}; equivalently, the exceptional setEx=VRn∥∖VxE_{x}=V^{\|}_{R^{n}}\setminus V_{x}has small measure. The heart of the subsection is
the perturbation argument of Claim7.20. For a fixedg=ρn​(v1,…,vn)g=\rho_{n}(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})and
most conjugating elementsp∈P1p\in P_{1}, we adjust theVV-coordinates one at a time:
